## Page 1


Three-Dimensional Real Affine Lie Groups
T. Aït Aissa and S. El Bourkadi and M. W. Mansouri
Department of Mathematics, Faculty of Sciences, Ibn Tofail University
Analysis, Geometry and Applications Laboratory (LAGA)
Kenitra, Morocco
e-mail: tarik.aitaissa @uit.ac.ma
said.elbourkadi @uit.ac.ma
mansourimohammed.wadia@uit.ac.ma
Abstract
We classify all left-invariant real affine connections in dimension three.
Our approach reduces the three-
dimensional problem to a two-dimensional one by decomposing each left-invariant affine connection into a
two-dimensional part and an additional one-dimensional component. After characterizing all possible two-
dimensional left-invariant affine connections, we return to the three-dimensional setting to obtain a simplified
description of all three-dimensional left-invariant affine connections. We then explicitly solve the resulting
simplified quadratic equations and perform a refined analysis up to isomorphism, leading to a complete classifi-
cation. Furthermore, we determine several geometric and algebraic properties of these structures, including the
Novikov, associative, radiant, and bi-symmetric conditions, as well as geodesic completeness.
Keywords: Affine Lie groups, Flat Lie algebras, Flat torsion-free connection.
2000 MSC: 22E25, 17B30, 53B05.
1. Introduction
An affine structure on a manifold M is given by a maximal atlas of charts with values in Rn whose transition
maps are locally affine transformations of Rn. It is equivalent to endow M with a flat and torsion-free connection
∇. It is also equivalent to give a local diffeomorphism D from the universal cover e
M of M into Rn, called the
developing map, such that there exists a representation h of the fundamental group π1(M) into the group Aff(Rn)
of affine transformations of Rn, called the holonomy, satisfying, for all X in e
M and γ in π1(M),
D(γ · X) = h(γ)D(X).
If g is in Aff(Rn), the developing maps D and g ◦D are considered equivalent. The simplest examples are the
tori Tn, whose universal covering space is the affine space Rn. If D is a diffeomorphism, i.e., if ˜M is affinely
diffeomorph to Rn, then M is called complete.
A manifold with an affine structure naturally induces a differentiable structure. Additionally, affine manifolds
are characterized by flat geometric structures. More precisely, There is a one-to-one correspondence between
the affine structures and the flat torsion-free affine connections ∇on the manifold M. Such a connection is a
linear connection on the tangent bundle T M whose torsion tensor and curvature tensor vanish identically. That
is, for all vector fields X, Y, Z ∈X(M),
T ∇(X, Y) = ∇XY −∇YX −[X, Y] = 0,
(1)
and
R∇(X, Y)Z = ∇X∇YZ −∇Y∇XZ −∇[X,Y]Z = 0.
(2)
Riemannian-flat and Lorentz-flat manifolds are subclasses of affine manifolds. The existence of affine structures
is a fundamental problem. An affine structure in dimension two exhibits a fundamental topological property. A
closed surface admits an affine structure if and only if its Euler characteristic vanishes [23].
Lie group structures with left-invariant affine structures provide many examples of affine manifolds. An affine
structure on a Lie group G is left-invariant if the left-multiplication by g, Lg : G →G, is an automorphism of
the affine structure.

## Page 2


Suppose that G admits a left-invariant flat, torsion-free connection ∇on G. It follows that for any two left-
invariant vector fields X, Y ∈g, their covariant derivative ∇XY ∈g is left-invariant as well. Therefore, the
covariant derivative defines a bilinear product on g, given by
XY := ∇XY,
for all X, Y ∈g. Keeping it simple, we will write ∇XY as XY or X · Y. Due to local flatness and torsion-free
properties of ∇, we have by (1) and (2):
[X, Y] = X · Y −Y · X,
(3)
[X, Y] · Z = X · (Y · Z) −Y · (X · Z).
(4)
We can rewrite (4) using (3) as
(X, Y, Z) = (Y, X, Z),
where (X, Y, Z) denotes the associator of the three elements X, Y, and Z in g. Thus, (g, ·) is a left-symmetric
algebra (or simply an LSA), see [11, 26]. There are many names for left-symmetric algebras. Left-symmetric
algebras are also known as flat Lie algebras, Vinberg algebras, Koszul algebras, or quasiassociative algebras.
We refer the reader to the survey article [10] and its references for more information on left-symmetric algebras.
This context raises the following important question, also posed by Milnor [24] in his studies of fundamental
groups of complete affine manifolds: Which Lie groups (Lie algebras) admit left-invariant affine structures?
There is a special difficulty in this question for nilpotent Lie groups. Several evidences suggest that nilpotent
Lie groups admit left-invariant affine structures [12]. In fact, Milnor postulated that this holds even for solvable
Lie groups [24]. Recently, however, counterexamples were discovered [8]. Several nilmanifolds are not affine.
When the Lie group G is semisimple (also perfect), it does not admit left-invariant affine structures [17]. As a
concrete example of affine manifolds, symplectic Lie groups can be regarded as a fundamental class of affine
manifolds. Indeed, it is well known that every symplectic Lie group (G, ω) carries a torsion-free flat connection
∇ω on G, naturally associated with the symplectic structure (see [13, Theorem 6]). In addition, cosymplectic
Lie groups (or Lie algebras) constitute another class of affine manifolds; see [15].
Another problem related to affine structures is their classification. We cite here several classification results
in low dimensions for left-symmetric algebras (or flat Lie algebras): the two-dimensional case [22]; three-
dimensional left-symmetric algebras over the field C of complex numbers [5]; the abelian case [19]; four-
dimensional reductive Lie groups over R and C [2, 9]; Complete left-invariant affine structures on nilpotent Lie
groups up to dimension 4 [20]; and abelian left-symmetric algebras up to dimension 5 [14].
The paper follows the following structure. In Section 2, we introduce important definitions and notions in
the context of flat Lie algebras and their basic properties. Section 3 is devoted to flat Lie groups (algebras) and
the description of flat, torsion-free connections on this class of Lie algebras. We also provide several charac-
terizations of isomorphisms between flat, torsion-free connections. In Section 4, we begin by classifying all
torsion-free connections in dimension 2 and then proceed to the classification of three-dimensional real flat,
torsion-free connections. The appendix 5 contains detailed proofs of the classification of flat, torsion-free con-
nections on abelian Lie algebra.
Notation and conventions. Unless otherwise stated, we work over a fixed field K of characteristic zero for
our results on affine Lie groups or flat Lie algebras. A geometric interpretation of simply connected Lie groups
over the field K = R of real numbers, is a natural one, which is therefore of principal interest in our investiga-
tions. Throughout this paper, the notations (g, ∇) and (g, ·) will be used interchangeably to denote the flat Lie
algebras, where ∇is flat, torsion-free connection on g, and “ · ” is defined by X · Y = ∇XY, for all X, Y ∈g.
2. Flat Lie algebras
First, we define flat Lie algebras, as a first definition, we begin with the notion of linear connection. Let G be a
connected and simply connected finite-dimensional Lie group with Lie algebra g.
A connection on a Lie algebra g is a bilinear map
∇: g × g →g,
(X, Y) 7→∇XY.

For all X, Y, Z ∈g, the torsion tensor T = T ∇is defined by
T(X, Y) = ∇XY −∇YX −[X, Y],
and the curvature tensor R = R∇by
R(X, Y)Z = ∇X(∇YZ) −∇Y(∇XZ) −∇[X,Y]Z.
A connection ∇is said to be torsion-free if its torsion vanishes, that is,
T = 0.
The curvature tensor R vanishes if and only if the map
τ∇: g →End(g),
X 7→∇X,
defines a representation of g on itself. In this case, the connection ∇is said to be flat.
Definition 1. A flat Lie algebra is a Lie algebra g equipped with a flat, torsion-free connection ∇. The pair
(g, ∇) is then called a flat Lie algebra.
We recall the following fundamental definition.
Definition 2. Let ∇1 and ∇2 be two flat, torsion-free connections on the Lie algebra g. Then, ∇1 and ∇2 are
isomorphic if and only if there exists a Lie algebra automorphism Ψ : g →g such that
∇2
X = Ψ ◦∇1
Ψ−1(X) ◦Ψ−1,
for all X ∈g.
(5)
Setting ∇XY = X · Y, we define the left and right multiplication operators associated with the product “ · ” by
LX(Y) = X · Y = ∇XY,
RX(Y) = Y · X = ∇YX,
for all X, Y ∈g. Moreover, the left-multiplication operator
L : g −→End(g),
X 7→LX,
defines a representation of the sub-adjacent Lie algebra of the flat Lie algebra.
There is a one-to-one correspondence between left-invariant affine structures on G and flat structures “·” (LSA-
structures) on g [26].
There are some very important subclasses of flat Lie algebras (Left-symmetric algebras):
Definition 3. Let (g, ·) be a flat Lie algebra.
1. If, for every X ∈g, the operator RX is nilpotent, then g is said to be transitive or complete. Transitivity
corresponds to the completeness of an affine manifold.
2. If, for every X, Y ∈g, RX ◦RY = RY ◦RX, then g is called a Novikov algebra.
3. If, for every X, Y, Z ∈g, the associator (X, Y, Z) is right-symmetric, that is,
(X, Y, Z) = (X, Z, Y),
then g is said to be bi-symmetric.
4. If there exists a vector ζ ∈g such that
∇Xζ = X,
for every vector X ∈g, then (g, ·) is called a radiant flat Lie algebra.
Remark 1.
1. The sub-adjacent Lie algebra of a complete left-symmetric algebra is solvable, (see [20, 25]).
2. The notion of Novikov algebra was introduced in connection with Poisson brackets of hydrodynamic type
and Hamiltonian operators arising in formal variational calculus, (see [4]).
3. The notion of bi-symmetric coincides with that of an assosymmetric ring in the study of nearly associative
algebras, (see, [21], [7]).
4. The notion of radiant manifolds was introduced in [16, 18]. Its existence is related to a cohomological
obstruction to the existence of a fixed point for the holonomy representation. The classification of radiant
flat Lie algebras up to dimension 3 was established in [1].

4. Classification of flat Lie algberas in low-dimensions
In what follows, we outline the main approach to classifying real flat Lie algebras in low dimensions. The
classification of real flat Lie algebras is particularly difficult, as there is no general theory to simplify or solve
the quadratic equations arising from the curvature condition of ∇. This stands in contrast to the case of flat
Lie algebras over the complex field C; see [5], where Lie’s theorem can be applied to obtain upper-triangular
representations associated to the flat torsion-free connection ∇. Our approach aims to simplify these quadratic
equations and solve them in a systematic manner. We begin with a torsion-free connection ∇0 on a Lie algebra
g0, which we extend to a torsion-free connection ∇as described in (8). This approach provides information
about the components of ∇relative to the basis of g = Rℓ⋉g0.
For example, if g is a three-dimensional solvable Lie algebra, then g is isomorphic to either Rℓ⋉R2 or Rℓ⋉
aff(1, R). This shows that g0 is either aff(1, R) or R2. The classification of torsion-free connections on aff(1, R)
or R2 is straightforward due to their low dimensions. The remaining equations (conditions (i), . . . (vi)) then
become easier to solve.
The classification of torsion-free connections on g0 = aff(1, R) or g0 = R2 is carried out modulo the following
equivalence relation: two connections ∇1 and ∇2 are equivalent if there exists an automorphism Φ ∈Aut(g0)
satisfying condition (c4) of Lemma 3 such that
∇2
x = Φ ◦∇1
Φ−1(x) ◦Φ−1,
∀x ∈g0.
(29)
for all x ∈g0. Now, if Rℓacts trivially on g0 (i.e., we have a central extension), then the equivalence relation
reduces to Condition (29) without requiring any condition on the automorphism Φ ∈Aut(g0); see Remark 2.
Every two-dimensional Lie algebra g has a flat, torsion-free connection. The following classification is easily
verified for dimension reasons based on the definition of flat, torsion-free connections.

4.1. Three-dimensional real flat Lie algebras
It is well known that there is no flat torsion-free connetion on a semisimple Lie algebra. Therefore, over the real
field R, besides 3-dimensional simple Lie algebras sl2(R) and so(3), up to isomorphisms, there are the following
(non-isomorphic) Lie algebras (Mubarakzyanov’s classification, we only give the nonzero products):
3g1, abelian.
3g2,1 ⊕g1 = ⟨e1, e2, e3 | [e1, e2] = e1⟩, decomposable solvable.
g3,1 = ⟨e1, e2, e3 | [e1, e2] = e3⟩, Heisenberg-Weyl algebra, nilpotent.
g3,2 = ⟨e1, e2, e3 | [e1, e3] = e1, [e2, e3] = e1 + e2⟩, solvable.
g3,3 = ⟨e1, e2, e3 | [e1, e3] = e1, [e2, e3] = e2⟩, solvable.
g3,4 = ⟨e1, e2, e3 | [e1, e3] = e1, [e2, e3] = αe2, −1 ≤α < 1, α , 0⟩, solvable, Poincaré
algebra p(1, 1) when α = −1.
g3,5 = ⟨e1, e2, e3 | [e1, e3] = βe1 −e2, [e2, e3] = e1 + βe2, β ≥0⟩, solvable.

For the remaining cases, i.e., ∇j, j = 1, 2, 3, 4, the corresponding connections are isomorphic under certain
conditions. In order to obtain an equivalence classification, it is not necessary to determine their representatives
up to isomorphism.

solutions to the flatness equations are given in Appendix 5, since this case is comparatively simple. Furthermore,
as will be seen in the subsequent analysis, the non-flat torsion-free cases reduce, up to isomorphism, to the study
of the flat torsion-free case.

Finally, a straightforward computation shows that the connections listed in Lemma 8 on the flat Lie algebra
g3,5 are pairwise non-isomorphic, except for certain cases that are not taken into consideration, since the corre-
sponding isomorphisms hold only locally and not globally.
Based on the assumptions in Lemma 8, it can be observed that the connections under consideration are not flat.
As part of the proof, only conditions are derived that guarantee that the resulting connections are not isomorphic
pairwise. Our aim is merely to establish an equivalence class and not a classification up to isomorphism, so this
requirement is not essential for our purposes.

Let G be a connected and simply connected affine Lie group. The following result summarizes our classification:
G is complete, Novikov, radiant, associative or bi-symmetric if it admits a left-invariant affine structure carrying
these properties.
Theorem 1. Let G be a connected and simply connected three-dimensional real affine Lie group. Then the
following statements hold:
1. G is complete;
2. G is Novikov Lie group;
3. G is radiant;
4. Associative Lie groups:

Ψ(e3) = x e1 + (b23 −c33)e3,
where x ∈R∗is a suitable parameter. Applying this automorphism to the connection given in (496) yields the
following equivalent connection
∇e1e1 = δe1, ∇e1e2 = ∇e2e1 = δe2, ∇e1e3 = ∇e3e1 = δe3, ∇e2e2 = −e1, ∇e2e3 = ∇e3e2 = −e3,
∇e3e3 = e3,
δ = 0, 1.
(497)
Note that if δ = 0, then the upper block of this matrix coincides with Case 1, where ∇0 ≡0. If δ = 1, then this
case also coincides with Case 4. Therefore, no further analysis is required.

Ψ(e2) = −a32e1 + e3,
Ψ(e3) = e1.

∇e2e3 = ∇e3e2 = e3,
∇e3e3 = −e2.
